The enormous combinatorial complexity belongs naturally to the enclosing multiplicity structure. Pairwise force laws can be extraordinarily useful approximations for reading particular physical consequences of that structure. But the primitive mathematical object is not necessarily the sum of pairwise causes. It is the joint count domain. This is exactly the kind of distinction Part I\'s microstate definition was designed to make.

### 4.40 Superposition Is a Property of the Projected Field Description
In linear physical regimes, conventional field theories often permit superposition. For example,

$$\mathbf{E} = \sum_{\alpha}\mathbf{E}_{\alpha}.$$

This mathematical representation is enormously useful. R²D should not interpret it as proof that the enclosing relation is literally assembled from independent field substances emitted by each peer. The sum is a property of the physical projection in the regime where that projection is linear. The underlying count domain remains the joint compatibility structure. Thus:

$$\text{field~superposition} \neq \text{proof~of~bottom-up~primitive~causation}\text{.}$$

The physical equation and the causal ontology are separate questions.

### 4.41 Field Variation Is the Locally Readable Part of Enclosing Structure
A perfectly uniform common relation can become background-like over a sufficiently local comparison. A varying relation remains distinguishable. This provides a general field principle. For two neighboring peers,

$$X_{a}$$

and

$$X_{b}$$

represent local field reads. If

$$X_{a} = X_{b},$$

the field relation is common over that comparison. If

$$X_{a} \neq X_{b},$$

the difference

$$\Delta X_{ab}$$

is locally readable. Thus:

$$\text{local~field~observability} \propto \text{distinguishable~variation~across~peer~reads}.$$

Gravity makes this obvious through tidal differences. Electromagnetism retains additional local distinguishability through charge orientation even in otherwise uniform fields.

### 4.42 Field Geometry Has Not Yet Been Defined
A field relation can now be understood without specifying why its magnitude changes with spatial distance. Nothing in the present chapter requires

$$r^{-1}, r^{- 2}, r^{- 3},$$

or any particular physical geometry. That question belongs to the physical read of the enclosing boundary. The next chapter asks what spatial coordinate is natural for an enclosing relation. The answer will be area. Only then will the familiar

$$r^{- 2}$$

laws of gravity, electrostatics, and radiation appear. This ordering prevents the radial coordinate from being mistaken for the boundary that it merely parameterizes.

### 4.43 Principle — Shared-Boundary Field
**Principle -- Shared-Boundary Field**

Let

$$\left\{ B_{n,\alpha} \right\}$$

be peer local boundaries contained within an enclosing boundary

$$B_{n + 1}.$$

A formal state at $B_{n + 1}$ is a compatible joint realization of those peers. Therefore, the shared relation among the peers belongs first to the enclosing boundary:

$$B_{n + 1} \Rightarrow \left\{ B_{n,\alpha} \right\}.$$

When this enclosing relation acquires a domain-specific physical representation over the peer domain, that representation is a field. Thus:

$$\text{field} = \text{physical~read~of~shared~enclosing~compatibility}\text{.}$$

### 4.44 Principle — Local Coupling and Enclosing Field
**Principle -- Local Coupling and Enclosing Field**

A local coupling belongs to one readable boundary. A field belongs to the enclosing relation among peers. The local physical force read therefore has the general projected structure

$$\text{local~coupling} \times \text{enclosing~field~read} \rightarrow \text{local~force~read}\text{.}$$

Gravity realizes this as

$$\mathbf{F}_{g} = m\mathbf{g}\mathbf{,}$$

while electrostatics realizes it as

$$\mathbf{F}_{e} = q\mathbf{E}\mathbf{.}$$

These are conventional physical laws, not R²D identities.

### 4.45 Principle — Applied and Gravitational Constraint
**Principle -- Applied and Gravitational Constraint**

The same local boundary

$$B_{n,a}$$

can participate in two distinct mechanical constraint contexts. Applied force

$$F_{a}$$

is a differential constraint imposed upon that selected boundary. Gravitational force

$$F_{g,a}$$

is the local mechanical projection of the shared enclosing constraint acting on the same boundary as one peer among many. Therefore:

$$m_{i} = m_{g}$$

because the local boundary is the same, while

$$F_{a} \neq F_{g}$$

because the constraint relation is different.

### 4.46 Principle — Applied and Gravitational Acceleration
**Principle -- Applied and Gravitational Acceleration**

Applied acceleration is a differential change of one local boundary relative to its immediate enclosing background. Gravitational acceleration is the projected read of the shared peer relation relative to a larger background. Thus:

$$a = \text{peer~relative~to~background}\text{,}$$

whereas

$$g = \text{peer~ensemble/background~relative~to~larger~background}\text{.}$$

A uniform gravitational realization can therefore be locally common mode while applied acceleration remains locally distinguishable.

### 4.47 Principle — Orientation-Even and Orientation-Retaining Fields
**Principle -- Orientation-Even and Orientation-Retaining Fields**

Gravity and electromagnetism occupy the same shared-boundary projection class but act on different local coupling structures. Gravity couples to the orientation-even read

$$m.$$

Electromagnetism couples to the orientation-retaining read

$$q = \sigma \mid q \mid .$$

Therefore:

$$\begin{aligned}
\text{gravity} & = \text{field~projection~of~shared~relation~among~orientation-even~peers}, \\
\text{electromagnetism} & = \text{field~projection~of~shared~relation~among~orientation-retaining~peers}.
\end{aligned}$$

Their physical laws differ because their local coupling structures differ.

### 4.48 Corollary — Pairwise Interaction Is a Projection
When the enclosing boundary is omitted, only the correlated peers remain visible. The shared relation is then represented as direct interaction:

$$B_{n,a} \leftrightarrow B_{n,b}.$$

The pairwise force can be an exact or highly accurate physical description. The EP error occurs only if that projected pairwise interaction is promoted into the primitive causal source of the shared organization.

### 4.49 Corollary — Free Fall Is Common-Mode Gravitational Realization
When a local object and its measuring apparatus participate in the same sufficiently uniform gravitational relation,

$$\Delta g_{local} = 0.$$

No differential applied acceleration need be detected. Thus:

$$\text{free~fall} = \text{local~common-mode~realization~of~the~enclosing~gravitational~relation}\text{.}$$

Support creates the differential constraint measured by an accelerometer.

### 4.50 Corollary — Tidal Gravity Is the Local Distinction of the Gravitational Field
For neighboring peers,

$$\Delta\mathbf{g}_{ab} = \mathbf{g}_{b} - \mathbf{g}_{a}$$

is the locally readable gravitational difference. Uniform $g$ can become locally background-like. Variation in $g$ cannot. This is the physical starting point from which gravitational curvature will later become a useful projected geometry.

### 4.51 Corollary — Electromagnetic Neutrality Is an Enclosing Read
If

$$Q = \sum_{\alpha}q_{\alpha} = 0,$$

the enclosing boundary has no net orientation-weighted charge read even though subordinate charged boundaries remain. Thus, neutrality is recursive cancellation of the leading oriented coupling, not destruction of subordinate charge.

### 4.52 Corollary — Electric and Magnetic Components Belong to One EM Relation
The decomposition into

$$\mathbf{E}$$

and

$$\mathbf{B}$$

depends on physical frame. Therefore, they should not be treated as two independent primitive R²D structures. They are physical coordinate components of one electromagnetic enclosing relation. Their exact transformation laws belong to conventional electromagnetism and relativity.

### 4.53 What Has Been Established
Chapter 3 considered one local boundary. Chapter 4 has placed that same boundary among peers. That single change fundamentally alters the physical problem. At the enclosing boundary,

$$B_{n + 1},$$

a state is not the state of one independently complete object. It is a classification of compatible joint peer realizations. Therefore, the common relation among the peers belongs first to the enclosing boundary. The causal structure is:

$$B_{n + 1} \Rightarrow \left\{ B_{n,\alpha} \right\}.$$

When that shared relation acquires a physical representation over the peer domain, it is read as a field. Thus, a field is not required as a primitive mediator connecting objects that were complete before the relation existed. The local objects and the field are complementary reads of one larger organization. The distinction between local coupling and enclosing field then makes gravity and electromagnetism structurally parallel.

For gravity,

$$m$$

is the orientation-even local coupling read, and

$$g$$

is the physical read of the shared gravitational relation. Conventional mechanics writes

$$F_{g} = mg.$$

For electromagnetism,

$$q = \sigma \mid q \mid$$

is the orientation-retaining local coupling read, and

$$E$$

is the physical read of the shared electromagnetic relation. Conventional electrostatics writes

$$F_{e} = qE.$$

The recursive architecture is therefore:

$$\text{local~coupling} \times \text{enclosing~relation} \rightarrow \text{local~force~read}\text{.}$$

This makes clear why the meaningful gravitational distinction is not between inertial and gravitational mass. The same local boundary is being read in both cases. The meaningful distinction is between the constraint imposed on that boundary individually and the constraint acting on it as one peer among many.

Thus:

$$F_{a}$$

and

$$F_{g}$$

belong to different recursive relations. Applied acceleration and gravitational acceleration likewise differ. Applied acceleration is differential motion of one peer relative to its immediate background. Gravitational acceleration is the projected read of the shared peer relation relative to a larger background. This explains why uniform gravity can become common mode in free fall.

A freely falling object and its local accelerometer participate in the same enclosing relation. No differential applied constraint exists between them. Support creates that differential relation and therefore becomes locally measurable. What remains locally observable from gravity itself is variation across peers:

$$\Delta g.$$

This is tidal structure.

Electromagnetism differs because its local coupling retains orientation. The same enclosing field acts differently on

$$q > 0, q < 0, q = 0.$$

Its local acceleration therefore depends on

$$q/m,$$

and cannot gene rally become common mode for all peers. This difference between gravity and electromagnetism follows not because one is a field and the other is somehow more geometric, but because the local couplings entering their shared-boundary projections carry different count structure.

Gravity is orientation even.

Electromagnetism retains orientation.

The electric and magnetic components of the electromagnetic field likewise need not be treated as separate primitive entities. They are frame-dependent physical decompositions of one shared electromagnetic relation.

A field can also remain stationary. Nothing in this chapter yet requires radiation. A stable enclosing compatibility relation projects as a stable field.

Radiation requires the next recursive operation: a change in the shared relation must become an occurrence, and that occurrence must recur.

Before developing that dynamics, however, one geometric question remains.

If a field belongs to an enclosing boundary, what physical spatial coordinate naturally reads that boundary?

Conventional gravity and electromagnetism answer with expressions containing

$$r^{- 2}.$$

But a radius is not a boundary. An area is. The next chapter therefore begins with the enclosing boundary itself and asks why its physical projection repeatedly appears through area.

## Chapter 5 — The Areal Boundary: Inverse-Square Fields, Kepler, and the Horizon
### 5.1 A Radius Is Not a Boundary
Chapter 4 established a shared-boundary interpretation of fields. Peer boundaries

$$\left\{ B_{n,\alpha} \right\}$$

participate in one enclosing count domain

$$B_{n + 1}.$$

A state at $B_{n + 1}$ is a classification of compatible joint peer realizations. The field is the physical read of that shared enclosing relation. Nothing in that construction required a radial coordinate. Nothing required

$$r^{- 2}.$$

Nothing required Euclidean space. The field relation existed first because the enclosing boundary existed first.

This exposes a basic problem with the conventional two-body picture. Two objects are usually drawn as

$$a\text{\:\,}\overset{ r }{\leftrightarrow}\text{\:\,}b.$$

The line connecting them becomes the primitive geometry. The corresponding force law is then written as a function of that separation.

R²D reverses the order. The primitive count relation is

$$B_{n + 1}^{ab} \supset \left\{ B_{n,a},B_{n,b},\ldots \right\}.$$

The physical separation

$$r$$

is a coordinate assigned later to that enclosing relation. A radius therefore cannot be the boundary. It parameterizes the physical geometry through which the boundary is read.

### 5.2 The Formal Boundary Is Not an Area
The distinction between R²D and its physical projection must remain explicit. A formal R²D boundary

$$B_{n}$$

defines a count domain. It does not primitively possess units of length or area.

Part I defines boundary, multiplicity, distinguishability, recurrence, and closure before spatial or temporal coordinates are introduced. Space and time are later physical readings of those count relations. Therefore:

$$B_{n} \neq \mathcal{A}_{n}.$$

But once an enclosing physical relation acquires a spatial read, a question arises: What spatial coordinate naturally represents an enclosing boundary?

For the field projections developed in Chapter 4, the answer is area. Introduce

$$\mathcal{A}$$

as the physical areal read of an enclosing spatial boundary. Thus:

$$B_{n + 1}\overset{\text{spatial~physical~read}}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{A}_{n + 1}.$$

The formal boundary remains a count domain. Area is its canonical spatial projection.

### 5.3 Why Area Comes Before Radius
A radius does not enclose. An area does. This becomes especially clear when a shared relation is approximately isotropic. Choose a radial physical coordinate

$$r$$

for an isotropic enclosing boundary. Its area is

$$\mathcal{A}(r) = 4\pi r^{2}.$$

The conventional explanatory sequence is usually:

$$r \rightarrow r^{2} \rightarrow 4\pi r^{2}.$$

The R²D projection reverses it:

$$\text{enclosing~relation} \rightarrow \text{areal~boundary~read} \rightarrow \text{radial~parameter~}r.$$

The square appears because a two-dimensional enclosing surface is being described through a one-dimensional radial coordinate. Thus:

$$r^{2}\text{~is~secondary~to~}\mathcal{A}.$$

### 5.4 The Canonical Spatial Boundary Read Is Areal
This chapter will therefore use the following principle: When an enclosing R2D boundary acquires a spatial field read, its canonical enclosing coordinate is area.

This does not mean that every R²D boundary in biology, chemistry, markets, or other domains is literally a geometric surface. It is a statement about the spatial projection. Nor does it mean that every physical boundary must be spherical. Spherical symmetry supplies the special relation

$$\mathcal{A} = 4\pi r^{2}.$$

The more primitive physical statement is simply that the enclosing coordinate is areal. Its particular relationship to local distance coordinates depends upon the physical geometry.

### 5.5 Gravity Reveals the Hidden Area
Conventional Newtonian gravity writes

$$g = G\frac{M}{r^{2}}.$$

Using

$$\mathcal{A} = 4\pi r^{2},$$

this becomes

$$g = 4\pi G\frac{M}{\mathcal{A}}.$$

The geometric content is therefore

$$g \propto \frac{1}{\mathcal{A}}.$$

Only after the area is parameterized by radius does this become

$$g \propto \frac{1}{r^{2}}.$$

Thus, the inverse-square law is not fundamentally an inverse-square statement. It is an **inverse-area relation written in radial coordinates**.

### 5.6 Electromagnetism Has the Same Areal Geometry
For an isolated spherically symmetric electric source, conventional electrostatics gives

$$E = \frac{1}{4\pi\epsilon_{0}}\frac{Q}{r^{2}}.$$

Again use

$$\mathcal{A} = 4\pi r^{2}.$$

Then

$$E = \frac{Q}{\epsilon_{0}\mathcal{A}}.$$

Thus:

$$E \propto \frac{1}{\mathcal{A}}.$$

Gravity and electrostatics therefore have the same spatial projection structure:

$$\begin{aligned}
g & \propto \frac{M}{\mathcal{A}}, \\
E & \propto \frac{Q}{\mathcal{A}}.
\end{aligned}$$

Their coupling structures differ. Their physical calibration constants differ. Their underlying physical theories differ. Their enclosing spatial projection is the same.

### 5.7 The Common $r^{- 2}$ Is Geometry, Not Common Substance
This distinction matters. The common appearance of

$$r^{- 2}$$

does not imply that gravity and electromagnetism are the same physical interaction. Chapter 4 already identified their crucial difference. Gravity couples to an orientation-even boundary read:

$$m.$$

Electromagnetism couples to an orientation-retaining boundary read:

$$q = \sigma \mid q \mid .$$

The common factor

$$r^{- 2}$$

therefore belongs to a different part of the physics. It belongs to the spatial representation of the enclosing boundary. Thus:

$$\text{different~coupling~structure} + \text{same~areal~projection} \rightarrow \text{different~inverse-square~fields}\text{.}$$

The exponent does not identify the ontology. It identifies the geometry.

### 5.8 The Inverse-Square Law Is a Projection Law
The general structure can now be written

$$\text{field~read} = \text{calibration} \times \frac{\text{enclosing~coupling~read}}{\mathcal{A}}.$$

For gravity,

$$g = 4\pi G\frac{M}{\mathcal{A}}.$$

For electrostatics,

$$E = \frac{Q}{\epsilon_{0}\mathcal{A}}.$$

When

$$\mathcal{A} = 4\pi r^{2},$$

both become inverse-square laws. Therefore, inverse-square behavior is the radial projection of inverse-area field readability. It is not itself a primitive force law.

### 5.9 Nothing Has to Be Physically Diluted
The familiar language of inverse-square fields encourages an image of something emitted by a source and diluted through increasing volumes of space. That interpretation is unnecessary. Take two enclosing areas:

$$\mathcal{A}_{1} = 4\pi r_{1}^{2},$$

and

$$\mathcal{A}_{2} = 4\pi r_{2}^{2}$$

with

$$r_{2} > r_{1}.$$

Then

$$\mathcal{A}_{2} > \mathcal{A}_{1}.$$

For a fixed enclosing coupling read $K$,

$$\frac{K}{\mathcal{A}_{2}} < \frac{K}{\mathcal{A}_{1}}.$$

The local field coordinate becomes smaller because the same enclosing relation is being read over a larger area. No primitive substance needs to become thinner. The boundary read has expanded.

### 5.10 Gauss\'s Law Makes the Boundary Explicit
This interpretation is not foreign to conventional physics. Gauss\'s law for gravity can be written

$$\oint_{\mathcal{A}}\mathbf{g} \cdot d\mathbf{A} = - 4\pi GM.$$

For spherical symmetry,

$$g\mathcal{A} = 4\pi GM$$

in magnitude, giving

$$g = 4\pi G\frac{M}{\mathcal{A}}.$$

Electromagnetism gives

$$\oint_{\mathcal{A}}\mathbf{E} \cdot d\mathbf{A} = \frac{Q}{\epsilon_{0}}.$$

For spherical symmetry,

$$E\mathcal{A =}\frac{Q}{\epsilon_{0}},$$

so

$$E = \frac{Q}{\epsilon_{0}\mathcal{A}}.$$

The R²D claim is not that Gauss\'s laws have been derived from recursive counting. They have not. The significance is that conventional field theory itself exposes the areal structure that the radial inverse-square notation obscures. The physical field is naturally integrated over an enclosing boundary.

### 5.11 Field Flux Is a Boundary Read
The surface-integral formulation makes the hierarchy especially clear. A local field value such as

$$g(r)$$

or

$$E(r)$$

is a local physical read. The integrated relation

$$\oint_{}\mathbf{F} \cdot d\mathbf{A}$$

belongs to the entire enclosing surface. Thus:

$$\text{local~field~value} \leftrightarrow \text{enclosing~areal~relation}\text{.}$$

The field can be sampled locally. Its organizing relation is defined over a boundary. That is precisely the shared-boundary architecture of Chapter 4 translated into spatial physics.

### 5.12 $G_{n}$ and $\epsilon_{0}$ Belong to Calibration, Not Geometry
The areal formulation separates physical calibration from spatial geometry. For gravity,

$$g_{n} = 4\pi G_{n}\frac{M}{\mathcal{A}}.$$

For electrostatics,

$$E = \frac{Q}{\epsilon_{0}\mathcal{A}}.$$

The common factor is the enclosing area,

$$\mathcal{A.}$$

The calibration coefficients belong to the domain-specific physical projection. Thus:

$$\mathcal{A =}\text{shared~areal~geometry}\text{,}$$

while

$$G_{n} = \text{gravitational~calibration~at~}B_{n}$$

and

$$\epsilon_{0}^{-1} = \text{electromagnetic~calibration}\text{.}$$

The subscript on $G_{n}$ is important. Nothing in the primitive R²D architecture requires the dimensional gravitational calibration measured at one recursively realized background boundary to remain identical at every enclosing boundary. Within one common gravitational domain, $G_{n}$ can be effectively constant because all local peers are read against the same enclosing calibration.

Across recursively distinct gravitational backgrounds, however,

$$\boxed{G_{n} = G_{n - 1}}$$

cannot be assumed primitively. The gravitational geometry and the gravitational calibration must therefore be kept separate. The relation

$$\mathcal{A} = 4\pi r^{2}$$

describes the spherical Euclidean spatial projection. The coefficient

$$G_{n}$$

determines how the enclosing gravitational relation is physically calibrated within that projection. A change in one is not definitionally a change in the other. This distinction becomes essential when the same observed gravitational relation can be represented either by changing source strength, changing gravitational calibration, or changing effective geometry.

The corresponding question for electromagnetic calibration remains open. The present argument does not establish that

$$\epsilon_{0}$$

is boundary dependent or derive an electromagnetic scaling law analogous to the gravitational one. The general lesson is narrower: inverse-area geometry does not determine the dimensional calibration of the field read.

### 5.13 Gravitational Calibration Is Boundary Relative
At gravitational boundary $B_{n}$, write the physical areal relation as

$$g_{n} = 4\pi G_{n}\frac{M}{\mathcal{A}_{n}}.$$

The quantity

$$G_{n}$$

is the calibration relating the orientation-even mass read

$$M$$

to the gravitational field read

$$g_{n}$$

over the enclosing area

$$\mathcal{A}_{n}.$$

It is therefore not interpreted in R²D as a primitive attraction strength carried identically by every pair of masses throughout the recursive hierarchy. It belongs to the physical projection at a specified gravitational boundary. Within a domain whose peers share one enclosing background relation,

$$G_{n}$$

may be experimentally universal. That local universality follows naturally if every gravitational read in that domain is referenced to the same background scale. Thus:

$$\text{common~background} \rightarrow \text{common~gravitational~calibration}\text{.}$$

But boundary-relative universality is not the same claim as primitive universality.

Part III establishes that recursively enclosing background recurrence is compressed toward lower physical frequency: an enclosing background is recursively slower than the subordinate background through which it is realized.  If physical gravitational calibration depends upon that background recurrence, then a change of recursive boundary can carry a change in calibration:

$$f_{n}^{\Phi} \neq f_{n - 1}^{\Phi} \Rightarrow G_{n}\text{~need~not~equal~}G_{n - 1}.$$

The quantitative relation is not yet available at this point in Part IV.

Two additional physical calibrations are still required. Electromagnetic propagation will supply a relation between recurrence and length through

$$c,$$

and quantum recurrence will supply a relation between recurrence and energy through

$$h.$$

Only after those physical mappings have been established can the gravitational calibration be expressed in background-defined units. Chapter 10 will then show that, under an invariant dimensionless gravitational count calibration,

$$\frac{G_{n}}{G_{n - 1}} = \left( \frac{f_{n - 1}^{\Phi}}{f_{n}^{\Phi}} \right)^{2}.$$

The important result at the present stage is therefore structural: $\mathbf{G}$ **may be constant within a gravitational background without being constant across recursively distinct gravitational backgrounds**.

### 5.14 The Electromagnetic Calibration Occupies the Parallel Position
Electrostatics gives

$$E = \frac{Q}{\epsilon_{0}\mathcal{A}}.$$

Thus:

$$\epsilon_{0}^{-1} = \frac{E}{Q\mathcal{/A}}.$$

The quantity

$$\epsilon_{0}$$

therefore occupies the electromagnetic calibration position corresponding structurally to the gravitational role of $G$. This does not imply that

$$G$$

and

$$\epsilon_{0}$$

are the same physical constant. They have different dimensions and belong to different physical projections. The correspondence is structural:

$$\begin{aligned}
G & :\text{gravitational~areal~calibration}, \\
\epsilon_{0}^{-1} & :\text{electric~areal~calibration}.
\end{aligned}$$

The relation of electromagnetic calibration to electromagnetic background will be developed when radiation is introduced.

### 5.15 The Two-Body Problem Is Already an Area Problem
The usual two-body diagram emphasizes one line:

$$m_{a}\text{\:\,} - - - r - - - \text{\:\,}m_{b}.$$

R²D instead begins with the enclosing relation

$$B_{n + 1}^{ab} \supset \left\{ B_{n,a},B_{n,b} \right\}.$$

Its spatial field read can be represented through an enclosing surface

$$\mathcal{A}_{ab}.$$

The radial coordinate then parameterizes this surface. Thus:

$$\left\{ B_{n,a},B_{n,b} \right\} \subset B_{n + 1}^{ab}\overset{\text{spatial~read}}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{A}_{ab}\overset{\text{radial~parameter}}{\rightarrow}r.$$

The pairwise force law is therefore already a compressed representation of a larger areal relation.

### 5.16 The Area Need Not Literally Be Centered on a Primitive Source
This qualification is important. The spherical surface used in the conventional field description is usually centered upon a source mass or charge. R²D does not infer from that coordinate construction that the central object primitively produces the boundary. The source-centered coordinate is part of the physical projection. The more primitive count relation remains the compatibility of peers within the enclosing boundary. Thus:

$$\text{source-centered~coordinates} \neq \text{source-centered~primitive~causality}\text{.}$$

The coordinate can be extraordinarily useful without determining the direction of ontological explanation.

### 5.17 Spherical Symmetry Is a Special Case
The relation

$$\mathcal{A} = 4\pi r^{2}$$

requires spherical symmetry in an approximately Euclidean spatial representation. It is not the universal definition of physical area. For nonspherical systems, the boundary can have a different geometry. Therefore, the primitive physical statement is: field read is organized over an enclosing area.

The special statement

$$\text{field} \propto r^{- 2}$$

requires the additional symmetry

$$\mathcal{A \propto}r^{2}.$$

This distinction will become crucial in gravity, because increasingly strong gravitational organization eventually makes simple Euclidean radius an insufficient coordinate for the enclosing area.

### 5.18 Area Becomes Dynamic in a Bound Orbit
The elementary inverse-square relation reveals area indirectly. Bound planetary motion reveals it directly. Consider two gravitationally coupled peer boundaries in a bound orbit. Their joint physical state changes recurrently. The relative position returns. An orbital closure is realized. Kepler described this gravitational relation before Newton introduced a pairwise gravitational force.

That historical order is conceptually appropriate for R²D: the whole-system closure relation is observable before a subordinate causal mechanism is assigned to the projected objects. Part I explicitly notes this broader whole-system-before-mechanism pattern. 

The first important geometric fact is that the orbit closes. The second is that its closure is areal.

### 5.19 Kepler\'s First Law Defines a Closed Gravitational Geometry
For an ideal bound orbit, Kepler\'s first law gives an ellipse. Let its semimajor and semiminor axes be

$$a$$

and

$$b.$$

The area enclosed by one completed orbital path is

$$\mathcal{A}_{K} = \pi ab.$$

This is not the same physical area as the spherical field surface

$$4\pi r^{2}.$$

The two must not be identified. One is the enclosing surface coordinate used in a spherically symmetric field read. The other is the planar area traced by a bound orbital closure. Their common significance is more primitive: the gravitational relation continues to become readable through area.

### 5.20 The Area Around Two Masses Has Become a Closure Area
At the elementary field level, area appears statically:

$$g \propto \frac{1}{\mathcal{A}}.$$

In a bound orbit, the shared gravitational relation changes continuously through its local physical coordinates while remaining closed as a whole. The boundary read has therefore acquired recurrence. The progression is:

$$\text{enclosing~field~area} \rightarrow \text{recurrently~traced~orbital~area}\text{.}$$

Again, this is not a claim that one literal spherical surface physically transforms into an ellipse. It is a recursive progression in **what aspect of the enclosing gravitational relation remains directly readable**.

### 5.21 Kepler\'s Second Law Joins Area to Recurrence
Kepler\'s second law states:

$$\frac{d\mathcal{A}_{K}}{dt} = \text{constant}\text{.}$$

Equal physical times correspond to equal swept areas. But R²D does not treat $t$ as primitive. Physical time is a clock-calibrated read of recurrence. The structurally prior statement is therefore:

$$\text{equal~reference-clock~recurrence} \rightarrow \text{equal~swept~gravitational~area}\text{.}$$

Kepler II is remarkable because it places the two coordinates developed throughout R2D side by side:

$$\text{distinguishable~geometry} + \text{recurrence}\text{.}$$

The gravitational system remains in a regime where both can still be read.

### 5.22 Swept Area Is a Read of the Changing Peer Relation
The conventional orbital drawing uses a radius vector from one projected body to the other. As the orbital macrostate changes, that vector sweeps area. R²D gives this area a different ontological position. It is not a substance generated by the moving body. It is the geometric record of the changing joint positional relation of the peers within their shared gravitational boundary. Thus:

$$\text{changing~peer~relation} \rightarrow \text{swept~areal~read}\text{.}$$

One completed orbit closes the relation and completes one total swept area.

### 5.23 One Orbital Closure Sweeps One Complete Area
For one complete orbit,

$$\mathcal{C}_{orb},$$

the total swept area is

$$\Delta\mathcal{A}_{K\mathcal{,C}} = \pi ab.$$

If the physical orbital period is

$$T,$$

then the average areal rate is

$$\frac{\mathcal{A}_{K}}{T} = \frac{\pi ab}{T}.$$

Using

$$f_{orb} = \frac{1}{T},$$

this becomes

$$\frac{\mathcal{A}_{K}}{T} = \pi ab\text{ }f_{orb}.$$

This is a physical relation, not an R²D identity. But it is precisely the kind of physical read expected when distinguishability and recurrence remain jointly accessible.

### 5.24 Kepler II Exposes What Newton Hides
Newtonian notation gives

$$g = G\frac{M}{r^{2}}.$$

Area is hidden inside

$$r^{2}.$$

Kepler\'s second law instead writes area directly:

$$\frac{d\mathcal{A}}{dt} = \text{constant}.$$

Thus, the two physical descriptions emphasize different coordinates of the same gravitational domain.

Newton:

$$\mathcal{A \rightarrow}r^{2}.$$

Kepler:

$$\mathcal{A}\text{~remains~explicit}\text{.}$$

Kepler\'s description therefore sits unusually close to the areal boundary read.

### 5.25 Angular Momentum Is the Mechanical Projection of the Areal Relation
Conventional mechanics explains Kepler II through angular momentum. For an ideal central force,

$$L = mr^{2}\dot{\theta}$$

is conserved, and

$$\frac{d\mathcal{A}}{dt} = \frac{L}{2m}.$$

This is a valid and extraordinarily useful physical relation. R²D changes only the explanatory order. The observed areal closure comes first. Angular momentum is the mechanical coordinate through which that regularity is represented. Thus:

$$\text{areal~recurrence} \rightarrow \text{angular-momentum~description}\text{,}$$

rather than assuming that primitive angular momentum creates the possibility of an enclosing areal closure.

### 5.26 Kepler III Adds the Recursive Clock
Kepler\'s third law gives

$$T^{2} \propto a^{3},$$

and therefore

$$f_{orb} \propto a^{- 3/2}.$$

Larger bound orbital scales recur more slowly. Part III already established the generic recursive-clock direction: larger enclosing closure recurs more slowly than the subordinate closures through which it is realized. Kepler supplies a particular gravitational physical law for that scaling. R²D does not derive the exponent

$$- \frac{3}{2}.$$

The important structural correspondence is:

$$\text{larger~gravitational~closure} \rightarrow \text{larger~areal~read} + \text{slower~recurrence}\text{.}$$

This points directly toward the gravitational readability horizon.

### 5.27 Orbital Radius Is Not Recursive Boundary Index
A caution remains necessary. The physical semimajor axis

$$a$$

does not equal the recursive index

$$n.$$

A continuous family of possible orbital radii can exist within one physical theory. R²D boundaries classify count domains. Thus:

$$a_{orbit} \neq n.$$

Kepler supplies a physical realization of a general R²D trend. It does not provide a literal one-to-one mapping from orbital size to boundary number.

### 5.28 Kepler Is the Gravitational Hinge
Kepler occupies a unique position in the gravitational hierarchy. At the ordinary inverse-square field level, area is present but hidden inside radial coordinates. At the black-hole horizon, area remains readable while the corresponding enclosing directed recurrence has crossed the gravitational readability limit. Kepler lies between them.

Both area and recurrence are directly measurable. Thus, Keplerian orbital closure is the regime in which the canonical gravitational boundary read and its recurrence remain jointly visible. That makes Kepler II more than an interesting orbital theorem. It is the hinge between ordinary gravitational field readability and horizon readability.

### 5.29 The Areal Boundary Grows While Its Clock Slows
The physical trend can now be represented schematically:

$$\mathcal{A \uparrow }f \downarrow ,$$

This is not yet a universal quantitative gravitational law. It is the physical manifestation of the recursive-clock direction. At ordinary planetary scale:

$$\mathcal{A}$$

is large but recurrence remains readily measurable. At increasingly enclosing gravitational scale, the relevant recurrence becomes progressively less accessible relative to the spatial boundary read. At the horizon, the asymmetry becomes extreme. Area remains. Directed enclosing recurrence does not.

### 5.30 The Black-Hole Horizon Makes the Boundary Itself Dominant
The event horizon provides the limiting gravitational example. Let its physical area be

$$\mathcal{A}_{H}.$$

The Bekenstein-Hawking relation is

$$\Sigma_{H} = \frac{k_{B}\mathcal{A}_{H}}{4\mathcal{l}_{P}^{2}}.$$

Using the dimensionless entropy coordinate

$$S_{H} = \frac{\Sigma_{H}}{k_{B}},$$

gives

$$S_{H} = \frac{\mathcal{A}_{H}}{4\mathcal{l}_{P}^{2}}.$$

Thus:

$$\ln W_{H} = \frac{\mathcal{A}_{H}}{4\mathcal{l}_{P}^{2}}.$$

At the gravitational readability limit, logarithmic state count is physically readable through area. This is the culmination of the areal boundary progression.

### 5.31 Horizon Area Is Not Background
The distinction from Chapter 3 must be maintained. The horizon area

$$\mathcal{A}_{H}$$

is a distinguishable boundary read. Background is

$$b_{H}, \Phi_{H} = \ln b_{H},$$

with subordinate directional classification no longer readable at the boundary where it is background. Therefore,

$$S_{H} \neq \Phi_{H}.$$

Likewise, the Bekenstein-Hawking entropy is not the Second-Law background increment. The area law reads boundary multiplicity. Background describes a different realized count relation. These should not be merged merely because both become important near a readability horizon.

### 5.32 The Horizon Does Not Introduce Area
The horizon is often where area first becomes conceptually striking. R²D suggests the opposite historical reading. Area has been present all along. At the elementary field level:

$$g \propto \mathcal{A}^{-1}.$$At the orbital level:

$$\frac{d\mathcal{A}_{K}}{dt} = \text{constant}\text{.}$$At the horizon:

$$S_{H} \propto \mathcal{A}_{H}.$$

The horizon does not introduce a new coordinate. It reveals the boundary coordinate with the greatest purity after other aspects of the enclosing dynamics have become unreadable.

### 5.33 The Areal Progression
The gravitational sequence can therefore be summarized as

$$\text{hidden~area} \rightarrow \text{recurrent~area} \rightarrow \text{horizon~area}\text{.}$$

More explicitly:

**Newtonian field**

$$g = 4\pi G\frac{M}{\mathcal{A}}.$$

Area is hidden inside the local field strength.

**Keplerian closure**

$$\frac{d\mathcal{A}_{K}}{dt} = \text{constant}\text{.}$$

Area and recurrence are jointly readable.

**Horizon**

$$S_{H} = \frac{\mathcal{A}_{H}}{4\mathcal{l}_{P}^{2}}.$$

Boundary area remains directly readable as state-count geometry while the corresponding enclosing directed recurrence is not. The equations are not equivalent. Their common coordinate class is.

### 5.34 The Area Around Two Masses Has Expanded Into Horizon Readability
The phrase "area around two masses" can now be given precise meaning. At the smallest gravitational projection considered here, the shared relation between two local mass boundaries is represented through an enclosing field surface. That areal relation is compressed into the radial law

$$r^{- 2}.$$

In a bound orbit, the shared relation closes dynamically and its changing state is directly recorded through swept area.

At still larger gravitational readability scale, trajectory-based descriptions become progressively less complete while the enclosing boundary itself remains readable.

At the horizon, area becomes the dominant observable count coordinate.

Thus, the progression is not that one literal physical surface expands continuously from a Newtonian sphere into an orbital ellipse and finally into an event horizon.

It is: the enclosing gravitational relation becomes progressively more directly readable as area.

That is the recursive statement.

### 5.35 Planck Area Is a Calibration of Areal Count
The horizon relation introduces

$$\mathcal{l}_{P}^{2}.$$

The dimensionless count is

$$S_{H} = \frac{\mathcal{A}_{H}}{4\mathcal{l}_{P}^{2}}.$$

Thus Planck area provides the physical conversion needed to express gravitational area as a dimensionless count. This does not require interpreting the horizon as literally assembled from tiny squares of area

$$\mathcal{l}_{P}^{2}.$$

The minimal conclusion is:

$$\text{physical~area}\overset{\mathcal{l}_{P}^{2}}{\rightarrow}\text{dimensionless~gravitational~count}\text{.}$$

Planck area is therefore a calibration of gravitational areal readability. It is not a constituent substance of space.

### 5.36 Area Is More Primitive Than Radius Within the Spatial Projection
The hierarchy can now be stated clearly. The formal primitive is

$$B_{n}.$$Its spatial field read is

$$\mathcal{A.}$$

Under spherical symmetry, a radial coordinate is defined by

$$r = \sqrt{\frac{\mathcal{A}}{4\pi}}.$$Therefore:

$$B_{n}\mathcal{\rightarrow A \rightarrow}r$$

is the R²D projection order. This does not mean that physical observers must calculate radius from area. It means that radius is logically a coordinate of the enclosing spatial boundary rather than the primitive object that generates it.

### 5.37 Curvature Begins When One Calibration Can No Longer Represent the Enclosing Area
In a flat spherical spatial read,

$$\mathcal{A} = 4\pi r^{2},$$

where $r$ is the locally calibrated radial coordinate.

Suppose, however, that the gravitational relation at an enclosing boundary is calibrated by

$$G_{n}$$

while the observer continues to describe that relation using the subordinate calibration

$$G_{n - 1}.$$

The same observed field can be written either as

$$g = 4\pi G_{n}\frac{M}{\mathcal{A}}$$

or, keeping $G_{n - 1}$ fixed,

$$g = 4\pi G_{n - 1}\frac{M}{\mathcal{A}_{eff}}.$$

Therefore,

$$\mathcal{A}_{eff}\mathcal{= A}\frac{G_{n - 1}}{G_{n}}.$$

This is an exact algebraic equivalence at the level of the areal gravitational projection. A change in gravitational calibration can therefore be represented as a change in effective enclosing area. This does **not** mean that every difference between

$$G_{n}$$

and

$$G_{n - 1}$$

automatically creates curvature. If the ratio

$$\frac{G_{n}}{G_{n - 1}}$$

is constant throughout the physical domain, then the corresponding area relation differs only by one constant scale factor. That can be absorbed into a global recalibration.

Nontrivial geometry appears when the calibration ratio itself varies across the readable domain. Let

$$\zeta(r) = \frac{G_{n}(r)}{G_{n - 1}}.$$

Then

$$\mathcal{A}_{eff}(r) = \frac{\mathcal{A}(r)}{\zeta(r)}.$$

For spherical projection,

$$\mathcal{A}(r) = 4\pi r^{2},$$

so the effective areal radius is

$$R_{\mathcal{A}}(r) = \sqrt{\frac{\mathcal{A}_{eff}}{4\pi}} = \frac{r}{\sqrt{\zeta(r)}}.$$

If $\zeta = \text{constant},$ then $R_{\mathcal{A}}$ is simply proportional to $r$.

If $\frac{d\zeta}{dr} \neq 0,$ then the relation between locally calibrated radial distance and enclosing areal radius is no longer one global Euclidean rescaling.

The projected geometry must encode the mismatch. Thus, the more precise R²D sequence is

$$\text{changed~background~calibration} \rightarrow \text{changed~gravitational~calibration} \rightarrow \text{changed~area-distance~relation}\text{.}$$

When that relation varies across the physical domain, nontrivial geometric projection becomes necessary. Curvature is therefore not identified with $G_{n}$ itself. It is the geometric representation required when a gravitational relation calibrated differently across the enclosing domain is nevertheless represented using common local spatial standards.

**This is the point at which area becomes more primitive within the gravitational projection than the radial coordinate used to describe it.**

### 5.38 Spacetime Geometry Is Not the R²D Boundary
General relativity uses a metric

$$g_{\mu\nu}$$

to define spacetime interval and curvature. R²D uses boundary-relative counting. Therefore:

$$B_{n} \neq g_{\mu\nu}$$

Likewise:

$$\mathcal{A}_{n} \neq g_{\mu\nu}.$$

The areal physical read helps explain why geometry becomes central in gravity. It does not collapse recursive count structure into metric geometry. The two remain different mathematical languages.

### 5.39 Einstein Geometry Is the Mature Gravitational Projection
General relativity provides the mature physical description of gravitational relations when one globally flat spatial and temporal coordinate system is no longer sufficient. Its field equation is conventionally written

$$G_{\mu\nu} + \Lambda g_{\mu\nu} = \frac{8\pi G}{c^{4}}T_{\mu\nu}.$$

R²D does not derive this equation by assigning its formal count quantities to its terms. The metric,

$$g_{\mu\nu},$$

the Einstein tensor,

$$G_{\mu\nu},$$

the stress-energy tensor,

$$T_{\mu\nu},$$

and the coefficient

$$G$$

are all physical coordinates of the gravitational projection. The boundary architecture developed here suggests why geometry becomes necessary.

A shared gravitational relation is first read through an enclosing area. If the calibration relating that area to local mass and acceleration differs across the gravitational domain, one fixed Euclidean area-distance relation no longer represents all local reads consistently. The physical theory must instead encode the compatibility of those local reads in a nontrivial geometry. This gives three possible projected locations for a discrepancy between local gravitational calibration and larger-scale gravitational realization:

$$\begin{aligned}
& \text{effective~source~content}, \\
& \text{effective~gravitational~calibration}, \\
& \text{effective~geometry}.
\end{aligned}$$

These are not necessarily three primitive causes. They can be alternative coordinates used to represent one changed enclosing gravitational relation. This is especially important when $G$ is allowed to be boundary dependent.

R²D is **not** proposing that one can simply replace

$$G$$

with

$$G(x)$$

in an otherwise unchanged Einstein equation. The geometric identity

$$\nabla^{\mu}G_{\mu\nu} = 0$$

requires the complete projected theory to remain covariantly consistent. A variable physical calibration therefore cannot generally be inserted as an isolated coefficient while every other part of the field description is held fixed.

The R²D claim is more primitive: when the enclosing gravitational calibration changes, the complete physical projection may change with it. What conventional physics represents as additional source, modified metric, or altered effective coupling may therefore be different representations of the same omitted background-dependent gravitational organization. General relativity remains the empirical geometric law that any such mapping must reproduce in the regime where its description is successful.

The R²D boundary is not the metric. The changing calibration is not the curvature tensor. The proposal is that the metric and curvature are the physical geometry required to preserve gravitational compatibility when locally fixed coordinates no longer provide a complete read of the enclosing relation.

### 5.40 The Horizon Is Where Geometry Nearly Becomes the Whole Read
This clarifies why gravity appears increasingly geometric toward the horizon.

At ordinary scales, an observer can read:

-   local positions;

-   motion;

-   orbital recurrence;

-   field strength;

-   area.

As gravitational readability becomes increasingly one-sided, some of these dynamical reads become inaccessible relative to the enclosing scale.

Area persists.

At the horizon, state count itself is read through

$$\mathcal{A}_{H}.$$

Thus, geometry does not become primitive. It becomes increasingly dominant because fewer complementary recursive coordinates remain jointly readable. That is the gravitational horizon described in Part III.

### 5.41 Electromagnetic Field Area Does Not Become a Gravitational Horizon
The shared areal geometry should not be overextended. Electromagnetic inverse-square fields and gravitational inverse-square fields share the relation

$$\mathcal{A \propto}r^{2}.$$

That does not mean that an electromagnetic enclosing boundary necessarily develops into a black-hole horizon.

The horizon is a specific gravitational readability limit. The commonality is the spatial projection:

$$\text{enclosing~field~boundary} \rightarrow \text{area}\text{.}$$

The subsequent physical realization of that area depends on the domain. Gravity develops Keplerian and horizon forms. Electromagnetism develops radiative and gauge-field forms.

### 5.42 Radiation Will Reuse the Same Area
The areal argument is not limited to static fields. Suppose a physical source emits power

$$P$$

through an approximately isotropic enclosing surface. The intensity at the boundary is

$$I = \frac{P}{\mathcal{A}}.$$

Under spherical propagation,

$$I = \frac{P}{4\pi r^{2}}.$$

Thus radiation inherits the same inverse-area geometry. But this equation describes only how an already-defined radiative occurrence is distributed over an enclosing spatial boundary. It does not explain what radiation **is**. That requires the recursive occurrence architecture of the next chapter.

### 5.43 Static Field and Radiation Share Area but Not Recurrence
This distinction is important. A static field can satisfy

$$E \propto \frac{1}{\mathcal{A}}$$

without a radiative occurrence propagating through the boundary. Radiation can satisfy

$$I \propto \frac{1}{\mathcal{A}}$$

because recurring energy or occurrence reads cross successive enclosing areas.

The same geometry appears in both. The count relations differ. Thus:

$$\text{static~field} = \text{persistent~enclosing~relation}\text{,}$$

whereas

$$\text{radiation} = \text{recurrent~enclosing~occurrence}\text{.}$$

Area alone does not distinguish the two. Recurrence does.

### 5.44 The Same Geometry Can Support Different Physical Ontologies
The repeated appearance of

$$r^{- 2}$$

therefore has a simple explanation. Whenever a physical quantity is read per unit area of an approximately isotropic enclosing boundary,

$$\mathcal{A} = 4\pi r^{2},$$ the radial form will contain

$$r^{- 2}.$$

This can occur for:

$${\text{gravitational~field},
}{\text{electric~field},
}{\text{radiative~intensity}.}$$

The physical source quantity differs. The calibration differs. The dynamics differ. The projection geometry is the same. Thus, inverse-square laws identify an areal readability class, not a unique fundamental force class.

### 5.45 Principle — The Areal Spatial Boundary
**Principle -- The Areal Spatial Boundary**

An R²D boundary is a formal count domain. When an enclosing boundary acquires a spatial field read, its canonical enclosing coordinate is area:

$$B_{n + 1}\overset{\text{spatial~read}}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{A}_{n + 1}.$$

Under spherical Euclidean representation,

$$\mathcal{A} = 4\pi r^{2}.$$

The radial coordinate is therefore a parameterization of the enclosing area rather than the primitive boundary itself.

### 5.46 Principle — Inverse-Square Projection
**Principle -- Inverse-Square Projection**

If a physical field read is inversely distributed over an enclosing area,

$$X \propto \frac{1}{\mathcal{A}},$$

then spherical projection gives

$$X \propto \frac{1}{r^{2}}.$$

Thus inverse-square behavior is the radial representation of inverse-area readability. The result is geometric. It does not identify the physical ontology of the quantity $X$.

### 5.47 Principle — Gravitational Areal Progression
**Principle -- Gravitational Areal Progression**

Gravitational boundary readability becomes increasingly explicit through area. At the ordinary field level,

$$g \propto \mathcal{A}^{-1}.$$In Keplerian closure,

$$\frac{d\mathcal{A}_{K}}{dt} = \text{constant}\text{.}$$At the horizon,

$$S_{H} = \frac{\mathcal{A}_{H}}{4\mathcal{l}_{P}^{2}}.$$

These are different physical relations. Their common gravitational coordinate is area. Thus:

$$\text{hidden~area} \rightarrow \text{recurrent~area} \rightarrow \text{horizon~area}\text{.}$$

### 5.48 Principle — Keplerian Areal Closure
**Principle -- Keplerian Areal Closure**

A bound orbital system preserves both the enclosing areal read and recurrence. For a complete elliptical orbit,

$$\mathcal{A}_{K} = \pi ab,$$while Kepler\'s second law gives

$$\frac{d\mathcal{A}_{K}}{dt} = \text{constant}.$$

Because physical time is a recurrence read, Keplerian closure is the gravitational regime in which enclosing area and recurrence remain jointly measurable. It therefore forms the physical bridge between inverse-area field readability and recurrence-unreadable horizon-area readability.

### 5.49 Principle — Horizon Areal Readability
**Principle -- Horizon Areal Readability**

At the gravitational readability horizon, enclosing directed recurrence is no longer jointly readable with the surviving geometric distinction. Boundary state count remains physically readable through horizon area:

$$S_{H} = \frac{\mathcal{A}_{H}}{4\mathcal{l}_{P}^{2}}.$$

Area is not created at the horizon. The horizon exposes the areal boundary coordinate already present implicitly in ordinary gravitational field geometry.

### 5.50 Corollary — Gravity and Electrostatics Share Geometry, Not Coupling
Gravity satisfies

$$g = 4\pi G\frac{M}{\mathcal{A}},$$while electrostatics satisfies

$$E = \frac{Q}{\epsilon_{0}\mathcal{A}}.$$

Therefore, they share an inverse-area spatial projection. They differ in local coupling architecture:

$$m$$is orientation even, while

$$q = \sigma \mid q \mid$$

retains orientation. The common inverse-square exponent therefore follows from shared boundary geometry rather than identical physical ontology.

### 5.51 Corollary — Radiation Intensity Is Also an Areal Read
For isotropic radiation with total power $P$,

$$I = \frac{P}{\mathcal{A}} = \frac{P}{4\pi r^{2}}.$$

The inverse-square attenuation of intensity therefore belongs to the same areal projection class. This does not yet define radiation. It defines the spatial geometry through which a radiative occurrence is read.

### 5.52 Corollary — Curvature Is Required When Flat Area-Radius Projection Fails
The Euclidean relation

$$\mathcal{A} = 4\pi r^{2}$$

is a special physical geometry. When local distance coordinates can no longer organize enclosing gravitational area through this flat relation, a more general geometric description is required. Curvature is therefore the natural projected language for the non-Euclidean relation between local measurements and enclosing gravitational boundary geometry. R²D does not identify curvature with its formal boundary. It explains why curvature becomes the appropriate physical coordinate system.

### 5.53 What Has Been Established
Chapter 4 established that a field is a physical read of a shared enclosing relation among peer boundaries.

Chapter 5 has now identified the spatial coordinate through which such a boundary is naturally represented.

The formal R²D boundary is not an area. It is a count domain. But once an enclosing field relation acquires a spatial physical read, the natural enclosing coordinate is

$$\mathcal{A.}$$

A radial coordinate

$$r$$

parameterizes that area. Under spherical Euclidean projection,

$$\mathcal{A} = 4\pi r^{2}.$$

This immediately changes the interpretation of inverse-square laws. Newtonian gravity,

$$g = G\frac{M}{r^{2}},$$becomes

$$g = 4\pi G\frac{M}{\mathcal{A}}.$$Electrostatics,

$$E = \frac{1}{4\pi\epsilon_{0}}\frac{Q}{r^{2}},$$becomes

$$E = \frac{Q}{\epsilon_{0}\mathcal{A}}.$$

Thus both are inverse-area relations before they are inverse-square radial laws. Their common exponent does not identify a common fundamental substance. It identifies a common spatial projection. Gravity and electromagnetism remain different because their local boundary couplings remain different. Gravity couples to an orientation-even read,

$$m.$$

Electromagnetism couples to an orientation-retaining read,

$$q = \sigma \mid q \mid .$$

The geometry is shared. The coupling is not. The same areal structure becomes increasingly explicit within gravity. The elementary two-body field hides area inside $r^{2}$. A bound orbit makes area dynamic. Kepler\'s second law,

$$\frac{d\mathcal{A}_{K}}{dt} = \text{constant},$$

directly joins areal distinguishability to physical recurrence. Kepler is therefore the gravitational hinge. Area and recurrence are simultaneously readable. The system closes. The area swept by one complete orbit is

$$\mathcal{A}_{K} = \pi ab.$$

Larger orbital closure also recurs more slowly according to Kepler\'s third law,

$$T^{2} \propto a^{3}.$$

R²D does not derive this exponent. It identifies the structural direction:

$$\text{larger~areal~closure} \rightarrow \text{slower~enclosing~recurrence}\text{.}$$

At the black-hole horizon, this progression reaches the gravitational readability limit. The Bekenstein-Hawking relation,

$$S_{H} = \frac{\mathcal{A}_{H}}{4\mathcal{l}_{P}^{2}},$$

makes logarithmic boundary state count itself physically readable through area. The gravitational progression is therefore:

$$\text{hidden~area} \rightarrow \text{recurrent~area} \rightarrow \text{horizon~area}\text{.}$$

The phrase does not mean that a Newtonian Gaussian sphere literally grows into an orbital ellipse and then into a black-hole horizon. It means that the enclosing gravitational relation becomes progressively more directly readable through the same physical coordinate class: Area.

This also clarifies why geometry becomes increasingly important in gravity. A flat spherical projection satisfies

$$\mathcal{A} = 4\pi r^{2}.$$

When local radial coordinates can no longer represent enclosing area through this simple Euclidean relation, richer geometry is required. General relativity supplies that geometry. Its metric and curvature are not R²D primitives. They are the mature physical coordinates through which gravitational boundary compatibility is represented after simple radial projection is no longer sufficient.

The same areal geometry also appears in radiation. For a radiative power $P$,

$$I = \frac{P}{\mathcal{A}} = \frac{P}{4\pi r^{2}}.$$

But area alone does not tell us what radiation is. A static field and radiation can share the same enclosing geometry while differing fundamentally in recurrence. A static field is a persistent enclosing relation. Radiation requires that a change in that relation acquire occurrence meaning at the next boundary and recur.

The next chapter therefore adds the count operation missing from the present one. The boundary is already defined. Its field is already defined. Its area is already defined. Now the enclosing relation changes. And a local closure becomes readable as an occurrence within the next boundary.

## Chapter 6 — Recursive Radiation: From Local Closure to Enclosing Occurrence
### 6.1 A Field Is Not Yet Radiation
Chapter 4 defined a field as the physical read of a shared enclosing relation among peer boundaries. For electromagnetism,

$$B_{n + 1}^{EM} \supset \left\{ B_{n,\alpha} \right\},$$

and the enclosing boundary defines which joint realizations of the orientation-retaining peers are compatible. A stable enclosing relation can therefore produce a stable physical field:

$$\text{stable~enclosing~EM~compatibility} \rightarrow \text{persistent~EM~field~read}\text{.}$$

Nothing about this requires radiation. A static charge possesses an electric field. A stationary gravitational system possesses a gravitational field. Neither observation requires a recurrent physical occurrence propagating away from the local relation. Radiation therefore requires an additional count operation.

The shared relation must **change**. That change must become distinguishable as an occurrence at the receiving boundary. And that occurrence must recur. Thus:

$$\text{field} = \text{persistent~enclosing~relation}\text{,}$$

while

$$\text{radiation} = \text{recurrent~occurrence~of~change~in~an~enclosing~relation}\text{.}$$

This distinction is the starting point of the chapter.

### 6.2 Part I Already Defines the Required Cross-Boundary Operation
Let

$$\mathcal{C}_{n,a}$$

be a completed local closure at peer boundary

$$B_{n,a}.$$

Let

$$\chi_{n + 1}$$

denote the enclosing occurrence with which that closure is compatible. The causal relation is

$$\chi_{n + 1} \Rightarrow \mathcal{C}_{n,a}.$$

The enclosing occurrence defines the compatible local closure through which it is realized. But after the local closure is complete, its count meaning changes when read from the enclosing boundary. Part I denotes this recursive carry:

$$\mathcal{C}_{n,a}\overset{\text{recursive~carry}}{\Rightarrow}\chi_{n + 1}.$$

These arrows are not causal inverses. The first describes causal realization. The second describes boundary-relative reclassification. Part I explicitly distinguishes these two readings: the peer closure is the subordinate expression of the enclosing occurrence, while recursive carry tells how the completed local realization is counted after the boundary changes. Radiation will use exactly this architecture.

### 6.3 Radiation Does Not Mean That a Local Closure Pushes Something Upward
This distinction must remain explicit. It is tempting to write

$$\mathcal{C}_{n} \rightarrow \chi_{n + 1}$$

and interpret the arrow as saying that a local event generates a larger-scale event. That would restore bottom-up causal mechanics. R²D instead states:

$$\boxed{\chi_{n + 1} \Rightarrow \mathcal{C}_{n}}$$

causally,

while

$$\mathcal{C}_{n}\overset{\text{carry}}{\Rightarrow}\chi_{n + 1}$$

describes how one completed realization is read from the adjacent boundary.

Radiation therefore does not require a primitive substance to be emitted upward through the recursive hierarchy. It requires one recursively organized realization to possess different count meanings at adjacent boundaries.

### 6.4 Charge Supplies an Oriented Local Closure Class
Chapter 2 established the count structure underlying electric charge. A binary local state architecture can admit opposed closure orientations

$$\mathcal{C}_{n,a}^{+}, \mathcal{C}_{n,a}^{-}.$$

When the enclosing classification preserves their distinction,

$$\pi_{n + 1}\left( \mathcal{C}^{+} \right) \neq \pi_{n + 1}\left( \mathcal{C}^{-} \right),$$

orientation survives recursive replacement. The physical electromagnetic coupling can then be written

$$q_{a} = \sigma_{a} \mid q_{a} \mid , \sigma_{a} = \pm 1.$$

Charge therefore supplies something that the orientation-even mass projection does not: a persistent local orientation class.

This orientation belongs to the local boundary. The complete electromagnetic relation among charged peers belongs to the enclosing boundary.

### 6.5 A Static Charge Defines a Persistent Enclosing Relation
Consider a charged boundary whose orientation and physical position remain unchanged relative to the relevant enclosing relation. Its local coupling read is

$$q_{a}.$$

The enclosing electromagnetic compatibility is stable. The physical projection is therefore a stationary field relation:

$$\text{fixed~oriented~coupling} + \text{stable~enclosing~compatibility} \rightarrow \text{static~EM~field}\text{.}$$

For an ideal isolated charge, conventional electrostatics writes

$$\mathbf{E} \propto \frac{q}{r^{2}}\widehat{\mathbf{r}}.$$

Chapter 5 already interpreted the factor

$$r^{- 2}$$

as the radial read of an enclosing areal relation. No radiative recurrence is required. The field exists because the shared relation exists.

### 6.6 Uniform Motion Does Not Yet Supply the Required Change
Now allow the charged boundary to move with constant physical velocity relative to an inertial frame. Its electromagnetic field representation changes with frame. Electric and magnetic components can be mixed by a change of observer. But if the background-relative velocity state remains constant,

$$\Delta\mathbf{v}_{\Phi,a} = 0,$$

no new acceleration distinction is continually being created. The motion classification persists. Thus:

$$\text{constant-velocity~charged~boundary} \neq \text{continually~changing~enclosing~motion~relation}\text{.}$$

This is why acceleration becomes the important physical distinction for radiation. The charge need not merely move. Its enclosing motion relation must change.

### 6.7 Acceleration Changes the Relation of the Oriented Boundary to Its Enclosing Domain
Chapter 3 developed applied acceleration as a change in the enclosing motion read of one selected local boundary:

$$\mathbf{a}_{a} = \frac{d}{dt}\left( \mathbf{v}_{a} - \mathbf{u}_{n + 1}^{\Phi} \right).$$

Again, this is a physical mapping rather than a primitive R²D equation.

For an uncharged boundary, this changes its mechanical relation to the enclosing background. For a charged boundary, something additional happens. The same boundary carries an electromagnetic orientation:

$$\sigma_{a}.$$

Therefore, changing its positional/motion relation also changes the way that retained orientation participates in the shared electromagnetic compatibility relation.

The same local boundary contains both positional distinction and retained EM orientation. Acceleration couples them.

### 6.8 An Accelerating Charge Is a Coupled Closure Problem
A charged boundary should therefore not be represented primitively as

$$\text{point~charge} + \text{acceleration}.$$

The R²D structure is richer. At $B_{n,a}$, the boundary has a positional macrostate structure. It also belongs to one retained orientation class

$$\sigma_{a}.$$

A local positional closure changes its enclosing motion relation. Because that local boundary also carries electromagnetic orientation, the positional closure changes its participation in the enclosing electromagnetic relation. Schematically:

$$\left( \mathcal{C}_{n,a}^{x},\sigma_{a} \right)$$

is locally realized as one compatible expression of

$$\chi_{n + 1}^{EM}.$$

The enclosing occurrence now possesses an electromagnetic physical read. This is the R²D entry point to radiation.

### 6.9 The Parallel with an Accelerating Mass Is Exact at the Recursive Level
Chapter 3 established the mechanical sequence

$$\text{local~positional~closure}\overset{\text{recursive~read}}{\Rightarrow}\text{enclosing~motion~occurrence}\text{.}$$

Electromagnetic radiation has the same recursive architecture, but the local boundary additionally carries retained orientation:

$$\text{local~oriented~positional~closure}\overset{\text{recursive~read}}{\Rightarrow}\text{enclosing~EM~occurrence}\text{.}$$

Thus:

$$\begin{matrix}
\text{Mechanical~read} & & \text{Electromagnetic~read} \\
\mathcal{C}_{n}^{x} & & \left( \mathcal{C}_{n}^{x},\sigma \right) \\
\Downarrow & & \Downarrow \\
\chi_{n + 1}^{v} & & \chi_{n + 1}^{EM} \\
\text{motion~occurrence} & & \text{radiative~occurrence}
\end{matrix}$$

The physical reads differ. The recursive operation is the same. This is why the accelerating-mass construction was necessary before radiation could be understood.

### 6.10 Acceleration Does Not "Create the Field"
A charged boundary already participates in an electromagnetic enclosing relation before it accelerates. Acceleration therefore does not create electromagnetism. It changes an existing shared relation. Thus:

$$\text{charge} \rightarrow \text{persistent~field~relation}$$

and

$$\text{changing~charged~motion} \rightarrow \text{changing~field~relation}\text{.}$$

Radiation belongs to the second. This distinction removes the need to imagine a static electric field and electromagnetic radiation as two unrelated substances produced by the same charge. They are different physical reads of different realization states of one enclosing electromagnetic relation.

### 6.11 The Receiving Boundary Supplies the Electromagnetic Occurrence Domain
At the local boundary,

$$B_{n,a},$$

the detailed closure can remain readable. At the enclosing electromagnetic boundary,

$$B_{n + 1}^{EM},$$

that local path need not survive as the same object. Part I allows the completed closure to be reclassified according to its enclosing occurrence. Thus:

$$\mathcal{C}_{n,a}^{EM}\overset{\text{carry}}{\Rightarrow}\chi_{n + 1}^{EM}$$

The physically relevant question at $B_{n + 1}^{EM}$ is no longer: Which detailed local two-state path occurred?

It is: Did an electromagnetic occurrence of the enclosing class occur?

This is exactly the replacement operation required for a radiative event.

### 6.12 Electromagnetic Background Is the Nondirectional Receiving Count
Part I defines background as realized count whose relevant subordinate directional classification is no longer readable at the boundary where it is background. Apply this to the electromagnetic receiving boundary. Let

$$b_{n + 1}^{EM}$$

denote the realized electromagnetic count whose subordinate local orientation/path identities are not readable there as the same distinctions. Then

$$\Phi_{n + 1}^{EM} = \ln b_{n + 1}^{EM}$$

is its logarithmic background coordinate. A radiative occurrence

$$\chi_{n + 1}^{EM}$$

is distinguishable **within** this receiving electromagnetic count domain. Thus, the emerging physical architecture is

$${\text{local~oriented~closure~at~}B_{n}
}{\overset{\text{recursive~read}}{\Rightarrow}
}{\text{distinguishable~EM~occurrence~within~background~at~}B_{n + 1}.}$$

This is the precise sense in which electromagnetic radiation can be understood as a local closure becoming an occurrence within an enclosing electromagnetic background.

### 6.13 Background Does Not Cause Radiation as a Mechanical Medium
This formulation should not be mistaken for an ether theory. The formal background

$$b_{n + 1}^{EM}$$

is a count relation. It is not assumed to be a mechanical substance through which electromagnetic waves physically push. Likewise:

$${\Phi^{EM} \neq A_{\mu}.
}{\Phi^{EM} \neq F_{\mu\nu},}$$

and

$$\Phi^{EM} \neq c.$$

The background provides the receiving nondirectional count/reference structure. The electromagnetic occurrence is a distinguishable realization within that structure. The physical propagation law is supplied later by electromagnetism.

### 6.14 One Occurrence Is Not Yet Radiation
A single enclosing occurrence

$$\chi_{n + 1}^{EM}$$

is an event. Radiation requires recurrence. Within a suitable recurrence-comparison domain, let

$$N_{\chi^{EM}}$$

be the number of completed occurrences of the electromagnetic transition class. The recurrence of these occurrences can then acquire a physical frequency read. Thus:

$$\chi_{n + 1}^{EM} \rightarrow \chi_{n + 1}^{EM} \rightarrow \chi_{n + 1}^{EM} \rightarrow \cdots$$

produces the count structure required for

$$f, \omega, \phi.$$

The physical frequency does not exist before recurrence is read through a clock calibration. Therefore:

$$\text{radiation} = \text{recurrent~enclosing~EM~occurrence}\text{.}$$

### 6.15 Frequency Is a Physical Read of Radiation Recurrence
Let

$$N_{\chi}$$

be the number of recurrent EM occurrences counted during a common physical interval

$$\Delta t.$$

Then the physical frequency read is

$$f = \frac{N_{\chi}}{\Delta t}$$

The angular frequency is

$$\omega = 2\pi f.$$

These are physical coordinates. They are not identical to the formal R²D occurrence coordinate

$$\tau = \ln\nu.$$

The hierarchy remains:

$$\text{recurrence~count} \rightarrow \text{physical~clock~comparison} \rightarrow f.$$

This distinction will become crucial when quantum phase is introduced.

### 6.16 Phase Is a Representation of Recurrent Radiative Occurrence
Once an electromagnetic occurrence recurs, its cyclic physical read can be represented by phase. Write

$$\phi$$

for that phase coordinate. Then, under a chosen physical clock calibration,

$$\phi(t) = \phi_{0} - \omega t$$

for a simple monochromatic representation. The phase is not the underlying count structure. It is a coordinate describing where the recurring physical radiation read lies within a cycle. Thus:

$$\text{EM~recurrence} \rightarrow \text{phase~representation}\text{.}$$

The wave description comes after the recurrent occurrence has been defined.

### 6.17 Propagation Is a Physical Within-Boundary Read
Recursive carry and spatial propagation must not be conflated. The transition

$$\mathcal{C}_{n}\overset{\text{carry}}{\Rightarrow}\chi_{n + 1}$$

changes the count meaning of one recursively organized realization across adjacent boundaries. Once

$$\chi_{n + 1}^{EM}$$

has been defined as an electromagnetic occurrence, its physical propagation through the electromagnetic domain is described by the physical theory at that receiving boundary. Propagation therefore does not mean repeated recursive carry through

$$B_{n + 2},B_{n + 3},\ldots$$

for every meter traveled. That would confuse recursive scale with physical position. Instead:

$$\text{recursive~carry} = \text{change~in~count~meaning~across~scale}\text{,}$$

while

$$\text{propagation} = \text{physical~realization~of~the~EM~occurrence~across~the~receiving~spatial~domain}\text{.}$$

This distinction is essential.

### 6.18 Maxwell Propagation Is the Conventional Physical Law
Once the source-free radiative occurrence has a physical electromagnetic representation, conventional Maxwell theory supplies its propagation law. In vacuum, electric and magnetic components satisfy wave equations such as

$$\nabla^{2}\mathbf{E} - \frac{1}{c^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{E}}{\partial t^{2}} = 0,$$

and

$$\nabla^{2}\mathbf{B} - \frac{1}{c^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}\mathbf{B}}{\partial t^{2}} = 0.$$

R²D does not derive these equations by translating formal count variables into

$$\mathbf{E},\mathbf{B},c.$$

They belong to the physical electromagnetic projection. Their role in Part IV is to specify how the enclosing EM occurrence propagates once that physical read has been established.

### 6.19 The Speed $c$ Is a Physical Propagation Calibration
The wave equations introduce

$$c.$$

Within R²D,

$$c$$

must remain a physical constant of the projected electromagnetic/relativistic theory. It is not formal background.

It is not primitive recurrence. It is not mass. Thus,

$$c \neq \Phi,$$

and no direct count identity is assumed. The current architecture determines where $c$ appears: physical calibration of EM propagation. This same constant organizes relativistic compatibility:

**Principle --- Background Calibration and the Relativistic Speed**

Background supplies two complementary physical references. Its nondirectionality defines the zero against which directed velocity is read,

$$\mathbf{v}_{\Phi} = \mathbf{v} - \mathbf{u}_{\Phi},$$

while its recurrence and spatial reads define a propagation calibration

$$c_{\Phi} = \mathcal{l}_{\Phi}f_{\Phi}.$$

Relativistic motion is therefore naturally expressed through the dimensionless background-relative ratio

$$\beta_{\Phi} = \frac{\mid \mathbf{v} - \mathbf{u}_{\Phi} \mid}{c_{\Phi}}.$$

If recursive changes in background scale alter $\mathcal{l}_{\Phi}$ and $f_{\Phi}$ reciprocally,

$$\mathcal{l}_{\Phi} \propto \left( f_{\Phi} \right)^{-1},$$

then

$$c_{\Phi} = \text{constant}\text{.}$$

The invariant speed of light is then not an unexplained primitive velocity inserted into relativity. It is the invariant conversion between two boundary-dependent physical reads of the same recursively realized background.

### 6.20 Radiation Propagates Over the Areal Boundary
Chapter 5 already established the spatial geometry. For approximately isotropic radiation, total radiative power

$$P$$

is read across an enclosing area

$$\mathcal{A} = 4\pi r^{2}.$$

The intensity is

$$I = \frac{P}{\mathcal{A}} = \frac{P}{4\pi r^{2}}.$$

Thus:

$$I \propto r^{- 2}.$$

This is the same areal projection structure encountered in gravity and electrostatics. But the physical quantity is now different. It is flux of a recurrent radiative read across the enclosing spatial boundary.

### 6.21 Radiative Field Amplitude Is Not Inverse Square
An important distinction follows. For far-zone electromagnetic radiation, the radiative field amplitude behaves approximately as

$$E_{rad} \propto \frac{1}{r},$$

with

$$B_{rad} \propto \frac{1}{r}.$$

The intensity is quadratic in field amplitude:

$$I \propto E_{rad}^{2} \propto \frac{1}{r^{2}}.$$

Therefore Chapter 5\'s areal argument applies directly to **radiative flux or intensity**, not to the radiative field amplitude itself. This distinction is physically important:

$$\text{static~Coulomb~field~amplitude} \propto r^{- 2},$$

while

$$\text{radiative~EM~amplitude} \propto r^{-1},$$

and

$$\text{radiative~intensity} \propto r^{- 2}.$$

The same area appears through different physical coordinates.

### 6.22 The Poynting Vector Is the Physical Flux Read
Conventional electromagnetism writes the electromagnetic energy flux as the Poynting vector,

$$\mathbf{S} = \frac{1}{\mu_{0}}\mathbf{E} \times \mathbf{B}\mathbf{.}$$

For far-zone radiation,

$$\mid \mathbf{E} \mid \sim c \mid \mathbf{B} \mid ,$$

so

$$\mid \mathbf{S} \mid \propto E^{2}.$$

The total radiative power crossing an enclosing surface is

$$P = \oint_{\mathcal{A}}\mathbf{S} \cdot d\mathbf{A}\mathbf{.}$$

This is precisely the conventional physical representation expected from the areal boundary architecture.

R²D does not identify

$$\mathbf{S}$$

with recursive carry. The Poynting vector is the physical energetic flux coordinate of the radiative projection.

### 6.23 Physical Energy Transport Is Not Formal Count Transport
Electromagnetic radiation physically carries energy and momentum in conventional physics. R²D need not deny this. But the physical statement "energy crosses a surface" must not be confused with the formal statement that a subordinate count is transported upward between recursive boundaries.

Recursive carry is reclassification. It is not a material flow. Thus:

$$\text{physical~radiative~energy~transport} \neq \text{formal~recursive~carry}\text{.}$$

The former belongs to the physical electromagnetic projection. The latter belongs to the boundary-relative count architecture that permits that physical projection to be defined.

### 6.24 The Sign of Charge Reverses the Field but Not Radiated Power
The retained-orientation account of charge now makes a nontrivial physical prediction natural. Under

$$q \rightarrow - q,$$

the electromagnetic orientation reverses. The electric and magnetic radiation fields reverse correspondingly. But a scalar radiative power cannot depend upon the arbitrary choice of which orientation class is called positive. Therefore

$$P(q) = P( - q).$$

Radiated power must be orientation even. The simplest nontrivial dependence is therefore quadratic:

$$P \propto q^{2}.$$

This does not by itself derive the full radiation law. It explains why an orientation-insensitive scalar naturally loses the charge sign even though the field retains it.

### 6.25 Reversing Acceleration Direction Also Need Not Reverse Scalar Power
Acceleration is directional. For a given charge, reversing

$$\mathbf{a} \rightarrow - \mathbf{a}$$

changes the angular orientation of the radiative field. But total emitted power remains a nonnegative scalar. Therefore:

$$P\left( \mathbf{a} \right) = P\left( - \mathbf{a} \right)$$

for an isolated nonrelativistic point-charge magnitude. The leading analytic scalar dependence is consequently even in acceleration magnitude. The simplest nontrivial term is

$$P \propto a^{2}.$$

Again, this is a symmetry argument about the form of the physical projection. It is not a derivation of the quantitative radiation law.

### 6.26 The Larmor Law Is the First Quantitative Test
Conventional nonrelativistic electrodynamics gives the Larmor formula

$$P = \frac{q^{2}a^{2}}{6\pi\epsilon_{0}c^{3}}.$$

This equation is an especially useful test of the emerging architecture. It contains four structural features:

$${P = 0 \text{when~}a = 0,
}{P \propto q^{2},
}{P \propto a^{2},}$$

and radiative intensity subsequently satisfies

$$I \propto r^{- 2}.$$

The R²D architecture now gives reasons to expect all four qualitative structures:

-   no changing enclosing motion relation under uniform inertial motion;

-   orientation-even scalar dependence on charge;

-   orientation-even scalar dependence on acceleration;

-   areal dilution of radiative flux.

What has **not** been derived is the precise coefficient

$$\frac{1}{6\pi\epsilon_{0}c^{3}}$$

or the complete angular radiation pattern. Those remain quantitative physical burdens.

### 6.27 The Radiation Field Makes the Acceleration Dependence Explicit
The conventional far-zone electric radiation field of a nonrelativistically accelerating charge has the schematic form
